```json
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\nRandom     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx 0.9$--$1.7{\\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s020\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s020\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s025\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s030\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\\times}$, $4.8{\\times}$, and $2.0{\\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\\times}$, $2.2{\\times}$, and $1.2{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\\times}$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is right-skewed: the mean severity difference exceeds the median by a factor of $1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction (mean $\\Delta = 22.94$ vs.\\ median $\\Delta = 14.59$). The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction. Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training methodology (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning).}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct         & 0.04 & 0.24 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; 0.235 rounds to 0.24 at 2dp\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing paired $d_z$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.44 \\to 0.80$; Qwen: $0.28 \\to 1.02$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). Figure~\\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes the dual-model severity trajectory. Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d_z$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d_z \\geq 0.55$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and paired $d_z$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results for the difference statistic. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero (vertical dashed line), confirming significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports paired $d_z$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired $d_z$ and per-problem median difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ for the phase-dependent II (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, per-problem paired). DeepSeek: $n{=}49$ problems (1 excluded); Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems (4 excluded).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DeepSeek $d_z$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] & Qwen $d_z$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.44 (small) & 0.32\\; $[0.20,\\; 0.47]$ & 0.28 (small) & 0.05\\; $[-0.01,\\; 0.11]$\\textsuperscript{ns} \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015, bf16_qw_imp_s015; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n0.20 & 0.52 (medium) & 1.15\\; $[0.66,\\; 1.26]$ & 0.55 (medium) & 0.21\\; $[0.07,\\; 0.40]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s020, bf16_qw_imp_s020; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n0.25 & 0.68 (medium) & 3.97\\; $[2.83,\\; 5.29]$ & 0.89 (large) & 1.11\\; $[0.61,\\; 1.47]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025, bf16_qw_imp_s025; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n0.30 & 0.80 (large) & 9.94\\; $[8.39,\\; 16.65]$ & 1.02 (large) & 2.62\\; $[1.72,\\; 3.65]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s030, bf16_qw_imp_s030; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{ns}CI spans zero; marginal at $s{=}0.15$ (bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nFor DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from small ($d_z{=}0.44$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d_z{=}0.80$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all bootstrap CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ excluding zero (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nFor Qwen, the compound interaction effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d_z \\geq 0.55$), suggesting a model-dependent onset threshold for detectable phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nAt higher sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.25$), Qwen exhibits larger standardized effect sizes than DeepSeek ($d_z{=}0.89$/$1.02$ vs.\\ $0.68$/$0.80$), consistent with lower between-problem variance in Qwen's II distribution yielding larger standardized differences despite smaller absolute II values. % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s025..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nFull per-problem distributions and permutation null distributions appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\nRandom     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx 0.9$--$1.7{\\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s020\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s020\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s025\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s030\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\\times}$, $4.8{\\times}$, and $2.0{\\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\\times}$, $2.2{\\times}$, and $1.2{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\\times}$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is right-skewed: the mean severity difference exceeds the median by a factor of $1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction (mean $\\Delta = 22.94$ vs.\\ median $\\Delta = 14.59$). The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.15$ & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.20$ & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.25$ & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\nQwen2.5-7B     & 0.04 & 0.24 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; 0.235 rounds to 0.24 at 2dp\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning). DeepSeek-R1-7B = DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B; Qwen2.5-7B = Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing paired $d_z$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.44 \\to 0.80$; Qwen: $0.28 \\to 1.02$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). Figure~\\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes the dual-model severity trajectory. Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d_z$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d_z \\geq 0.55$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and paired $d_z$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds.     "oldString": "\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\n\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\nWe present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\\S\\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Overview}\n\\label{sec:exp-overview}\n\nWe evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\\S\\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.\nModels are evaluated in \\texttt{bfloat16} precision (\\S\\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.\n\n\n\\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}\n\\label{sec:strategy-severity}\n\nThe compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}\n\\label{tab:aggregate-ii}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nImportance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\nRandom     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}\n\\label{sec:phase-amplification}\n\nDecomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \\S\\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.\n\nUnder importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \\approx 0.9$--$1.7{\\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} / \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}\n\\label{tab:main-results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Strategy & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{mid}}$ & $\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}}$ & $\\Delta$ & $G$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\\mathbf{69}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015\n0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015\n\\addlinespace\n0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\\mathbf{103}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s020\n0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s020\n\\addlinespace\n0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\\mathbf{222}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025\n0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s025\n\\addlinespace\n0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\\mathbf{274}$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030\n0.30 & Random     & 2.503 & 2.592 & 4.041 & 1.54 & 1.6 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s030\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nThe gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\\times}$, $4.8{\\times}$, and $2.0{\\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\\times}$, $2.2{\\times}$, and $1.2{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig1_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\\times}$ to $274{\\times}$, while random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\\times}$. Shaded regions denote 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:hero}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe report medians throughout because the II distribution is right-skewed: the mean severity difference exceeds the median by a factor of $1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction (mean $\\Delta = 22.94$ vs.\\ median $\\Delta = 14.59$). The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\\S\\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586\n\n\n\\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}\n\\label{sec:crossover}\n\nThe phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \\emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_crossover.pdf}\n\\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28\n\\label{fig:crossover}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \\emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.\n\nThe crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62\n\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nTo assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.\n\nDespite lacking explicit \\texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\\S\\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\\Delta = \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{low}} - \\widetilde{\\mathrm{II}}_{\\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction.}\n\\label{tab:cross-model}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.15$ & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.20$ & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.25$ & \\footnotesize $s{=}0.30$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek-R1-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\nQwen2.5-7B     & 0.04 & 0.24 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; 0.235 rounds to 0.24 at 2dp\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\\columnwidth}@{}}{\\footnotesize Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training (distillation vs.\\ instruction tuning). DeepSeek-R1-7B = DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B; Qwen2.5-7B = Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nQwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing paired $d_z$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.44 \\to 0.80$; Qwen: $0.28 \\to 1.02$; Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). Figure~\\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes the dual-model severity trajectory. Spearman $\\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d_z$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d_z \\geq 0.55$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\nAs a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_crossmodel.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030\n\\label{fig:cross-model}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\\S\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Validation}\n\\label{sec:stat-validation}\n\nWe validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and paired $d_z$ for effect size.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation test.}\nFor each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\\Delta_i^{(\\text{strat})} = \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{low},i} - \\mathrm{II}_{\\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta} = \\text{median}_i\\!\\bigl(\\Delta_i^{(\\text{imp})} - \\Delta_i^{(\\text{rand})}\\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired permutation test (difference statistic) for compound severity: importance vs.\\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$ paired problems, $10{,}000$ permutations). $p$-values are Bonferroni-corrected for 4 comparisons.}\n\\label{tab:permutation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & Observed $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ & 95\\% CI & $p_{\\text{Bonf}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference\n0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference\n0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference\n0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\\,0.001$ \\\\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \\to 0.69 \\to 1.66 \\to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_bootstrap.pdf}\n\\caption{Forest plot of permutation test results for the difference statistic. Horizontal bars show 95\\% permutation CIs; diamonds mark observed median differences $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$. All CIs lie entirely above zero (vertical dashed line), confirming significant strategy-dependent severity at every sparsity level.}\n\\label{fig:permtest-forest}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Effect sizes.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens-d} reports paired $d_z$ (bootstrap $B{=}10{,}000$) measuring the standardized difference in II between low- and high-sparsity phases under importance-based eviction.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Paired $d_z$ and per-problem median difference $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ for the phase-dependent II (low $-$ high) under importance-based eviction. Bootstrap 95\\% CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ ($B{=}10{,}000$, per-problem paired). DeepSeek: $n{=}49$ problems (1 excluded); Qwen: $n{=}46$ problems (4 excluded).}\n\\label{tab:cohens-d}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n$s$ & DeepSeek $d_z$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] & Qwen $d_z$ & $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.15 & 0.44 (small) & 0.32\\; $[0.20,\\; 0.47]$ & 0.28 (small) & 0.05\\; $[-0.01,\\; 0.11]$\\textsuperscript{ns} \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015, bf16_qw_imp_s015; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n0.20 & 0.52 (medium) & 1.15\\; $[0.66,\\; 1.26]$ & 0.55 (medium) & 0.21\\; $[0.07,\\; 0.40]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s020, bf16_qw_imp_s020; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n0.25 & 0.68 (medium) & 3.97\\; $[2.83,\\; 5.29]$ & 0.89 (large) & 1.11\\; $[0.61,\\; 1.47]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025, bf16_qw_imp_s025; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n0.30 & 0.80 (large) & 9.94\\; $[8.39,\\; 16.65]$ & 1.02 (large) & 2.62\\; $[1.72,\\; 3.65]$ \\\\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s030, bf16_qw_imp_s030; per-problem paired B=10000; d_z from cohens_d_bf16.json + cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{ns}CI spans zero; marginal at $s{=}0.15$ (bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nFor DeepSeek, the effect size increases monotonically from small ($d_z{=}0.44$) at $s{=}0.15$ to large ($d_z{=}0.80$) at $s{=}0.30$, with all bootstrap CIs for $\\widetilde{\\Delta}$ excluding zero (Table~\\ref{tab:cohens-d}). % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nFor Qwen, the compound interaction effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \\geq 0.20$ ($d_z \\geq 0.55$), suggesting a model-dependent onset threshold for detectable phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nAt higher sparsity levels ($s \\geq 0.25$), Qwen exhibits larger standardized effect sizes than DeepSeek ($d_z{=}0.89$/$1.02$ vs.\\ $0.68$/$0.80$), consistent with lower between-problem variance in Qwen's II distribution yielding larger standardized differences despite smaller absolute II values. % exp_id: bf16_ds_imp_s025..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s025..s030; per-problem paired B=10000\nFull per-problem distributions and permutation null distributions appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap-details}.\n",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FY8NkYsoSACm4WHRAgMFEy`</summary>

```text
1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	We present our empirical findings in five parts: strategy-dependent compound severity (\S\ref{sec:strategy-severity}), phase-modulated amplification (\S\ref{sec:phase-amplification}), the phase-strategy crossover (\S\ref{sec:crossover}), cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}), and statistical validation (\S\ref{sec:stat-validation}).
6	
7	
8	\subsection{Experimental Overview}
9	\label{sec:exp-overview}
10	
11	We evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B using a 50-problem stratified subset of MATH-500 (\S\ref{sec:dataset}). For each problem, we measure perplexity under four conditions---dense, attention-only pruning, KV-only eviction, and compound (both)---at sparsity levels $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$. We compare importance-based and random eviction strategies (\S\ref{sec:sparsity-config}), and segment each generated sequence into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity reasoning phases via quantile-based attention sparsity partitioning (\S\ref{sec:phase-seg}). The interaction index (II, Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) quantifies compound super-additivity, and the severity gradient ($G$, Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}) captures phase-dependent amplification.
12	Models are evaluated in \texttt{bfloat16} precision (\S\ref{sec:experimental-setup}). Full experimental details appear therein.
13	
14	
15	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity (C1)}
16	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
17	
18	The compound severity difference reveals a stark dependence on eviction strategy. Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference $\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ across 50 problems for each strategy and sparsity level. Under importance-based eviction, compound sparsity produces substantial super-additive degradation that grows with sparsity: $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, a $37{\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, the severity difference shows no systematic growth, remaining near zero ($-0.02$ to $1.54$). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\small
23	\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}
24	\label{tab:aggregate-ii}
25	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
26	\toprule
27	Strategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\
28	\midrule
29	Importance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff
30	Random     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff
31	\bottomrule
32	\end{tabular}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	This strategy dependence indicates that compound sparsity severity is not an intrinsic property of the compression level. Rather, the same sparsity configuration produces qualitatively different compound behavior depending on the eviction strategy---an emergent phase-strategy interaction whose structure we unpack in the following subsections.
36	
37	
38	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification (C2)}
39	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
40	
41	Decomposing the interaction index by reasoning phase reveals that the strategy-dependent severity observed in \S\ref{sec:strategy-severity} is driven by amplification concentrated in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports per-phase median II and the severity gradient $G$ at each sparsity level.
42	
43	Under importance-based eviction, the severity gradient---the ratio of low-phase to high-phase median II---increases with sparsity from $69{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$. The low-sparsity phase---corresponding to exploratory reasoning with broadly distributed attention---bears disproportionate compound degradation, while the high-sparsity phase---corresponding to structured computation with focused attention---experiences near-zero compound interaction ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, the gradient remains near unity ($G \approx 0.9$--$1.7{\times}$) at all sparsity levels, confirming that phase modulation is specific to the emergent interaction between head pruning and importance-based scoring. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
44	
45	\begin{table*}[t]
46	\centering
47	\small
48	\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ is the median severity difference. $G = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} / \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ quantifies phase-dependent amplification.}

68	
69	The gradient growth is sub-exponential: successive ratios of the median severity difference $\Delta$ between adjacent sparsity levels are $3.8{\times}$, $4.8{\times}$, and $2.0{\times}$---a decreasing terminal growth rate consistent with a ceiling effect as compound degradation in the low-sparsity phase approaches saturation. The severity gradient $G$ exhibits the same pattern, with consecutive ratios of $1.5{\times}$, $2.2{\times}$, and $1.2{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this trajectory, contrasting the steep importance-based curve against the flat random baseline. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff
70

The median resists the influence of outlier problems with exceptionally long reasoning traces, while bootstrap confidence intervals (\S\ref{sec:stat-validation}) account for this skewness via the resampling distribution. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; source: cohens_d_bf16.json mean_diff=22.939, experiment_summary_table.json median_diff=14.586
79	
80	
81	\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover (C3)}
82	\label{sec:crossover}
83	
84	The phase-modulated gradient produces a striking qualitative phenomenon: a \emph{crossover} in which importance-based eviction is simultaneously the better and worse strategy depending on the reasoning phase. At $s{=}0.25$, importance-based eviction yields a lower II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}} = 0.032$ vs.\ $0.818$), but a higher II in the low-sparsity phase ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} = 7.196$ vs.\ $1.363$). This is not merely a magnitude difference---it is a reversal in the relative ranking of strategies across phases. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json
85	
86	Figure~\ref{fig:crossover} displays the crossover structure as a heatmap of per-phase II values across strategies and sparsity levels.
87	
88	\begin{figure}[t]
89	\centering
90	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_crossover.pdf}
91	\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; 7.196/1.363=5.28
92	\label{fig:crossover}
93	\end{figure}
94	
95	The crossover emerges from an emergent phase-strategy interaction: in the high-sparsity phase---where attention is already focused---importance-based eviction preserves genuinely critical positions, yielding lower compound severity than random eviction. In the low-sparsity phase, however, head pruning redistributes attention mass across surviving heads, altering the importance score landscape in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). The result is that targeted eviction inadvertently removes positions that would have been retained under the pre-pruning importance ranking---producing \emph{higher} compound severity than the explicitly random baseline.
96	
97	The crossover is present at all four tested sparsity levels ($s \geq 0.15$): at each level, importance-based eviction achieves lower II in the high-sparsity phase but higher II in the low-sparsity phase relative to random eviction. The magnitude of the low-phase reversal grows from $3.9{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $5.4{\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, before moderating to $3.6{\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ as random eviction severity also rises. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; ratios: 0.398/0.103=3.87, 1.496/0.276=5.42, 7.196/1.363=5.28, 14.639/4.041=3.62
98	
99	
100	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication (C5)}
101	\label{sec:cross-model}
102	
103	To assess whether the phase-modulated severity gradient generalizes beyond DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, we replicate the importance-based eviction experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone architecture (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.
104	
105	Despite lacking explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity across its generation trajectory to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\S\ref{sec:phase-seg}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).
106	
107	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model} compares the median severity difference across both models.
108	
109	\begin{table}[t]
110	\centering
111	\small
112	\caption{Cross-model replication: median compound severity difference ($\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$) under importance-based eviction.}
113	\label{tab:cross-model}
114	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
115	\toprule
116	Model & \footnotesize $s{=}0.15$ & \footnotesize $s{=}0.20$ & \footnotesize $s{=}0.25$ & \footnotesize $s{=}0.30$ \\
117	\midrule
118	DeepSeek-R1-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
119	Qwen2.5-7B     & 0.04 & 0.24 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; 0.235 rounds to 0.24 at 2dp
120	\bottomrule
121	\multicolumn{5}{@{}p{\columnwidth}@{}}{\footnotesize Both models share the Qwen2 architecture but differ in training (distillation vs.\ instruction tuning). DeepSeek-R1-7B = DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B; Qwen2.5-7B = Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct.}
122	\end{tabular}
123	\end{table}
124	
125	Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct qualitatively reproduces the monotonic severity gradient observed in DeepSeek. The median severity difference increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model}), paralleling DeepSeek's $0.39$-to-$14.59$ trajectory at approximately one-tenth to one-fifth the absolute magnitude. Both models yield strictly increasing paired $d_z$ trajectories (DeepSeek: $0.44 \to 0.80$; Qwen: $0.28 \to 1.02$; Table~\ref{tab:cohens-d}). Figure~\ref{fig:cross-model} visualizes the dual-model severity trajectory. Spearman $\rho = 1.0$ between the four-point $d_z$ sequences (gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. The Qwen effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, bootstrap CI includes zero), becoming reliable at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d_z \geq 0.55$); this onset lag relative to DeepSeek is consistent with the lower overall compound severity in instruction-tuned models lacking explicit reasoning structure. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107); cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json
126	
127	As a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size (Cohen's $d = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
128	
129	\begin{figure}[t]
130	\centering
131	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_crossmodel.pdf}
132	\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. Both DeepSeek (solid) and Qwen (dashed) exhibit monotonically increasing $\Delta$ with sparsity; Qwen's magnitude is approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030
133	\label{fig:cross-model}
134	\end{figure}
135	
136	The replication on a second model with distinct training methodology suggests that the phase-modulated severity gradient is not an artifact of DeepSeek's distillation-induced phase structure, but rather an emergent property of how compound sparsity interacts with attention dynamics in Transformer-based reasoning. We note that both models share the same backbone architecture; generalization to other architectures (e.g., LLaMA, Mistral) remains an open question (\S\ref{sec:limitations}).
137	
138	
139	\subsection{Statistical Validation}
140	\label{sec:stat-validation}
141	
142	We validate the strategy-dependent severity using two complementary approaches: a paired permutation test on the difference statistic for significance, and paired $d_z$ for effect size.
143	
144	\paragraph{Permutation test.}
145	For each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\Delta_i^{(\text{strat})} = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\widetilde{\Delta} = \text{median}_i\!\bigl(\Delta_i^{(\text{imp})} - \Delta_i^{(\text{rand})}\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.
146	
147	Table~\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. All four sparsity levels reject $H_0$ (no strategy difference) after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$), with observed median differences increasing monotonically from $0.37$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $9.85$ at $s{=}0.30$. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json
148	
149	\begin{table}[t]
150	\centering
151	\small
152	\caption{Paired permutation test for compound severity: importance vs.\ random eviction on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}49$, $10{,}000$ permutations, Bonferroni-corrected).}
153	\label{tab:permutation}
154	\begin{tabular}{@{}rrcl@{}}
155	\toprule
156	$s$ & Observed $\widetilde{\Delta}$ & 95\% CI & $p_{\text{Bonf}}$ \\
157	\midrule
158	0.15 & 0.37 & $[0.19,\; 0.62]$ & 0.0032 \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s015; source: permutation_test_results.json s015.permutation_test_difference
159	0.20 & 0.97 & $[0.69,\; 1.49]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s020; source: permutation_test_results.json s020.permutation_test_difference
160	0.25 & 3.34 & $[1.66,\; 6.64]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s025; source: permutation_test_results.json s025.permutation_test_difference
161	0.30 & 9.85 & $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ & ${<}\,0.001$ \\ % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest_s030; source: permutation_test_results.json s030.permutation_test_difference
162	\bottomrule
163	\end{tabular}
164	\end{table}
165	
166	The monotonic growth of both the point estimate and the CI lower bound ($0.19 \to 0.69 \to 1.66 \to 6.70$) confirms that compound severity amplification under importance-based eviction strengthens with sparsity---not merely in central tendency but also in worst-case lower bounds. Figure~\ref{fig:permtest-forest} presents these results as a forest plot. % exp_id: deepseek_bf16_permtest; source: permutation_test_results.json
167	
168	\begin{figure}[t]
```
